Will seemed to understand it. That the data and the feeling of driving the car could flow together. Could work together to bring about a better car. 

 

Like the one that made him a champion. 

 

He sighs and looks at his watch. Oscar’s late. 

 

He drops the ball and watches it bounce along the court before rolling to a stop at the net. 

 

“Hey,” a quiet, flat voice says. Lando watches as Oscar slinks into the room. “Sorry, got stuck in a bit of traffic after work.” 

 

It’s almost a shock to see Oscar like this. Not dressed in a garish papaya polo or an ironed white dress shirt. 

 

No, he’s dressed casually, fitted in a pair of gym shorts and a loose hoodie. His hair is tucked up under a baseball cap, and he drops his backpack unceremoniously on the bench next to their court. He offers Lando a tight smile. 

 

“No worries,” Lando says, stiffly.  “Ah, I brought you a racket.”

 

He gestures towards where two rackets rest against the net pole. 

 

“Thanks, man,” Oscar says as he throws his cap on top of his bag and pulls his sweatshirt over his head. 

 

“Yeah, uh,” Lando swallows as he watches more of Oscar’s figure be revealed by the slow removal of his outer layer. Leaving him in only a tight, grey compression shirt. “No problem.” 

 

It’s odd. Lando’s surrounded by built, attractive men day in and day out. George isn’t exactly his type, but he can admit he’s got an insane body. And he’s long gotten over feeling a bit smitten when Lewis or Carlos speak in those gentle, caring tones while looking at him with those sharp, handsome faces. 

 

It’s just…Oscar usually wears quite loose clothes. Unflattering team-issued polos and cargo shorts. Or stiff, business casual button-ups and pressed slacks with outdated quarter zips. 

 

Lando can’t be blamed for feeling such a shock at seeing Oscar’s lean, tight waist. The broad stretch of his shoulders and back. The pop of his, frankly unneeded, large biceps as he brushes his hair back and pops his hat back on. The outline of his pecs in the clinging fabric of his shirts. 

 

Fuck me, is that a six-pack? Lando wonders, noticing the faint outline of a compact stomach. 

 

Lando’s got a good body. Better than good. An athlete’s body, built by Jon’s diet and training. But Oscar?

 

 Oscar’s a fucking engineer, and he looks like he could go toe to toe with Lando. 

 

He leans down to retie his shoes, drawing Lando’s gaze to the dusting of hair on his legs and the constellations of freckles and spots on his pale, sinewy arms. 

 

He averts his gaze when Oscar leans back up and offers him the flat, thin line of his mouth. Lando supposes it might be a smile. 

 

“Right,” he says. “I was thinking we’d just go over the basics. Since you’ve never played before.” 

 

“I’ve played tennis before,” Oscar offers. “Are they similar?” 

 

“They share some things in common,” Lando says, shrugging. “Same at the core, but different in the details.” 

 

“Ah,” Oscar says, before laughing a bit to himself. “A bit like us, then?” 

 

“How do you figure?” 

 

Oscar shrugs and makes an exaggerated type of inward facial expression. “I mean, we both love racing, right?  Both wanna do the best job at the end of the day. Both work for McLaren. We just have different functions is all.” 

 

“Different functions are one way to put it,” Lando says, grabbing his own racket and a few balls, stuffing the extra into his pockets. Oscar grabs the other one and steps to the other side of the net. He takes a few moments to crack his neck. A long, thin, delicate sort of thing compared to Lando’s thick one. His fingers twitch at the sight. 

 

“Well, we all have the same goal in mind,” Oscar says, focusing Lando’s attention. “We both want to win.” 

 

Lando laughs. “Well, that’s for sure. We’re just going about it in opposite ways.” 

 

“What should I do?” Oscar says. For a moment, Lando wants to open his mouth and just tell him to stop being a fucking dickhead. But he’s standing diagonally from Lando, slightly crouched. 

 

“We’ll just do some rallies first,” Lando says, biting back his words. “It’s a four-player game, but we can start with the basics for today. Practice some serves and stuff for next time.”

 

“Oh, there’ll be a next time?” Oscar says, raising a brow. 

 

“You think we’re gonna solve all our shit in one go?” Lando asks. “We can have a heart-to-heart if you’d rather, Piastri?” 

 

“I’m good, but thanks,” Oscar says, blandly. Lando stops himself from rolling his eyes. 

 

He serves the ball, and they hit it back and forth in silence for a few moments. The only sound the ball slapping against their rackets and bouncing across the court. The occasional squeak of their trainers when one of them hits a bit too far back. 

 

It’s not bad. Oscar isn’t bad. But it’s probably much less talking than Andrea would like. 

 

It’s just..awkward. 

 

They’ve never really spoken outside of work. And Lando isn’t even sure what to talk about. 

 

And it’s not like Oscar is about to say anything either. 

 

Lando clears his throat as he waits for Oscar to return the ball to him. 

 

The sound practically echoes in the empty court. 

 

“So, uh,” Lando tries, before letting his words falter. Oscar looks at him expectantly. A light flush starts on his cheeks. 

 

“Ah, yeah,” Oscar says, opening his mouth before promptly closing it and avoiding eye contact with Lando. 

 

Ten minutes of back and forth must pass as Lando racks his brain for something to say. Something less related to racing or testing. Anything that’ll get Oscar talking. 

 

He thinks about commenting on the goddamn weather for a moment. Or the weird joke Zak made in their last meeting. Or what he had for lunch. 

 

“I think I get you more than you think,” Oscar says, breaking the silence.

 

“Huh?” Lando asks. “How’d you mean?” 

 

“Just,” Oscar starts. “I used to race, too. I know it feels like a lot under the helmet.”

 

Lando scoffs. Even when he’s trying to relate, he can’t help but be a prick. Lando hits the ball back hard, watching as Oscar is too slow to hit it. It hits the wall behind him and drops, rolling slowly back towards them. 

 

“F3, right?” he asks. 

 

Oscar nods. 

 

“Didn’t make it any further?” Lando prods, knowing he’s already being a bit of a dick. “No one wanted to sponsor you?” 

 

Oscar tenses across from him. A sort of freeze sets across all his muscles and limbs. He grits his teeth as he looks back at Lando. He looks away and takes a deep, heavy breath. 

 

“I had a sponsor,” Oscar says. “Alpine wanted to bring me into F2.”

 

“Alpine,” Lando says, crinkling his nose and imagining Oscar in a pink and blue suit. The image is unnatural, despite how unflattering their own orange may be.  “So why didn’t you then? Go to F2.” 

 

Oscar scrunches up his face. “I got injured.” 

 

“Injured?” 

 

“Yes, injured,” Oscar says, slowly, like Lando didn’t hear him the first time. He sighs and bounces the ball in his hand at his feet. “I had a bad crash. It fucked up my back. Not enough to leave me totally out of it, but enough that the doctors worried if I got into another crash, even a small one, I could become paralyzed.” 

 

“Oh,” Lando says lamely. Feeling a bit stupid and bad for poking Oscar. “Sorry, I didn’t know.” 

 

Oscar’s lips uptick in a half smile. “How would you know? You’d already been in F1 when I started. What would you care about a failed F3 driver?” 

 

“Hey,” Lando protests. “I keep up with some of the feeder stuff. Were you any good?” 

 

“Yeah,” Oscar says, casual like he isn’t describing most drivers’ worst nightmares. “Think I could have won the title that year, to be honest.”

 

Lando feels a bit stumped. He’d been aware that Oscar used to drive, but had figured he hadn’t been that good. Or that he’d failed to secure a seat as he progressed. Not that the choice has been taken out of his hands. That he’d had potential. 

 

It sends a shiver down Lando’s spine. 

 

It’s not like he’s ignorant of the danger of this sport. Seen bad crashes and injuries. Seen the way these injuries sit in the exhaustion and muscle loss of retired drivers. Has had his own fair share of accidents that left him a bit anxious the next time he got behind the wheel. 

 

But he always got back behind the wheel. No matter how badly his hands shook or how it felt like the car might be out of his control, Lando still mustered the courage to get back in. Had to get back in. This was his life. His dream. His very being. 

 

And Oscar had lost that. Had it ripped from his hands before his potential really took shape. 

 

“That sucks,” Lando says, knowing he sounds stupid. 

 

“It was just a collection of bad circumstances,” Oscar says, shrugging. “Too much speed from me. Too little from the other driver. And um… my engineer didn’t tell me that there was someone ahead, or like warn me to reduce my speed quickly enough. I hit the barrier pretty hard.” 

 

“Oh,” Lando says. He bites his lip. “And the other driver? What about them?” 

 

“Think he drives rally now,” Oscar says with a bit of a laugh. “He’s ok.” 

 

“And your back?” Lando asks. He looks at their rackets. “This wasn’t too much?” 

 

Oscar shakes his head. “Not at all. It’s good for me to move and keep my back strong. I try to maintain somewhat of the routine I had when I was still a driver. I keep up with my trainer from back then, and he gives me new workouts to try and stuff. Just can’t be driving with like crazy g-forces anymore.” 

 

He offers Lando an awkward smile. 

 

“Still, mate,” Lando says. “That’s hard. I can’t even imagine.” 

 

Oscar shrugs again. “It’s fine. I got to go to university, and I still get to be in racing. Even if I'm not in the car anymore, I still get to be on the track and stuff. I’m not behind the wheel, but I get to be in your ear.”

 

“That’s for sure,” Lando jokes, hoping to ease some of the weight of their conversation. Finding a way to believe that Oscar really is fine, like he says. 

 

Oscar looks like he’s thinking for a moment.

 

“I know you don’t like me, Lando,” he says. “But I’m just trying to make sure you have all the data. That you’re safe.” 

 

Lando taps his racket against his shin for a moment. “I get that, Oscar. But you can’t speak to me like a robot. Or like I’m an idiot. No matter what you think.”

 

“I’m not trying to.” 

 

“Yeah, but you sound so robotic,” Lando says. “I’m not saying you need to jump for joy every time I do an overtake. But some positivity would be nice.” 

 

“I’m not gonna pretend everything’s great if it isn’t,” Oscar says, firmly. “And I can’t change how my voice sounds just to appease your emotions.” 

 

His emotions. Oscar says it like it’s some terrible thing. Like it’s some sticky, messy monster that Lando carries on his back every day. Like he’s lesser for feeling things. 

 

“I’m not asking you to appease my feelings,” Lando scoffs. A part of him wants to pout like a child. 

 

“My work is data, Lando,” Oscar says, crossing his arms over his chest. “You want me to fake emotion so you feel better. That’s just not me. It’s not how I go racing. Data has no emotion. And neither does racing.” 

 

“That’s stupid,” Lando says before he can stop himself. Oscar looks at him expectantly. “Racing is emotion. Wins and losses and shit like that. You think Max goes from P17 to a win because of data? It’s because of determination. Because he cares about winning.” 

 

“You think GP didn’t help guide Max in that race?” Oscar says. “Not to mention Max is one of the best drivers, maybe ever. Skills don’t equal emotion.” 

 

“Ok, what about Abu Dhabi?” Lando asks. “Daniel fucked up, and I had to fight for that win on my own. You think I wasn’t anxious? Or determined? We wouldn’t be constructors champions without that win.” 

 

“What about Brazil then?” Oscar asks. “Or Austin? You made emotional decisions that almost cost you your championship.” 

 

“You’re impossible,” Lando says, rolling his eyes. “Sure, those weren’t great examples, but that’s my point. Emotion drives the good and the bad. I could have just given up all those times. Or even when we were in last place cars. Data can’t solve all of that.”

 

“And emotion can?” 

 

“It can drive people,” Lando argues. “What if we’d all just given up and thought ok well, we have a shit car, let’s just accept it? We’re only here because everyone cares. They had the drive and the want to win. To do better. Those are feelings. And they made us champions.” 

 

Oscar looks at him for a long moment, face carefully impassive. 

 

“Maybe, we should just agree to disagree,” Oscar says. 

 

“Look, I’m not trying to fight,” Lando says, pinching the bridge of his nose. “I’m trying to get you to see my point of view. I want us to have a good relationship here. Like, I’m not trying to fuck up both our lives.”

 

“I’m not either,” Oscar says, quietly. Soft and unsure, like Lando wasn’t even meant to hear it. 

 

“I know,” Lando concedes, even if he’s not sure he believes it. “But I need you to meet me in the middle a bit. If this is gonna work?” 

 

“I can’t change the way I talk,” Oscar says. “This is who I am.” 

 

He knows Oscar means it plainly. But it hits Lando square in the chest. 

 

Right, he’s a proper cunt then, isn’t he? The guy who’s spent years being told to change who he is is throwing it right back into Oscar’s face. 

 

“I know,” Lando says. “I didn’t mean it that way.” 

 

“It’s fine,” Oscar dismisses, like he’s used to people not liking who he is. It makes Lando feel like shit. “We can meet in the middle. What does that look like for you?” 

 

He asks it like Lando is an equation he can’t figure out. An Excel formula that’s coming back #N/A. 

 

But maybe that’s what he is. Oscar’s own enigma. 

 

Well, Lando can provide a bit of data on himself. 

 

“I don’t see our listening issues being solved in one go,” Lando says, making Oscar nod in agreement. “But if you can just give me a bit more encouragement. Or even just pointing out when I’ve done something well, so I know what’s working. That could be a good start.” 

 

Oscar mulls the words over in his head for a moment. His face pinches up before he lets it relax and nods. “Alright, I can try it.” 

 

“Thank you,” Lando says. “What can I do for you?” 

 

“Can you just…try to listen to the words?” Oscar says. “Like the actual words, not the tone. I can’t just change how I talk, but if you can just try to take my words at face value, then maybe it won’t sound so robotic.” 

 

Lando looks at Oscar. For the first time, he notices the little ticks Oscar has. Easier to do when they’re alone. In the quiet of a padel court and away from the buzz of the factory or the paddock. Oscar shifts on his feet. He brings his hand up and bites at his nails. He avoids Lando’s gaze fully. 

 

“Ok,” Lando says. “I can try. I can’t… promise I’ll do it perfectly in the heat of the moment.” 

 

“That’s alright,” Oscar concedes. “I can’t say I’ll be great at the encouragement thing right off the bat.” 

 

Lando waves his hand in the air. “Making a little progress together is better than doing the same thing repeatedly, trying to achieve our separate versions of perfection.” 

 

A snort comes out of Oscar unbidden.

 

“What lame self-help book did you get that from?” Oscar says.

 

“I got it from my therapist,” Lando says honestly, fighting a smidge of embarrassment. “But it’s true. Anyways, and you promise you'll listen if the car doesn’t feel right to me and talk to the engineers?” 

 

“I’ll listen,” Oscar says. It’s not a promise exactly, but it’s better than nothing, Lando supposes. Progress over perfection. 

 

“Great,” Lando says. “Then I can report to Andrea that we made some progress.”

 

Oscar gives him a sharp nod and another flat smile. 

 

“Should we shake on it?” Oscar asks. Lando sighs, fully exasperated. 

 

“How professional of you, Mr. Piastri,” Lando says.

 

“I think Oscar’s fine,” he says, rolling his eyes and extending his hand. 

 

Lando walks over and grasps his hand firmly, noticing how he easily eclipses Oscar’s smaller, thinner hand. “Thanks for the teambuilding sesh, Osco.”

 

----

 

Oscar can’t get comfortable. The rest of his family has long since gone to bed, but he hasn’t been able to stop turning and shifting in his bed. 

 

Usually, he welcomed the time away from England and basked in the warmth of his home. Of the easy teasing and affection from his mum and sisters.  Of the lipstick kisses and cheek pinches from his nan. Of the way his dad eyes his team-issued backpack and suitcase with a sort of pride. 

 

Different from how he used to look in the after years. The years Oscar spent trying to get back to being a normal human and burying himself in books. Different from the way he’d looked at Oscar like a bad investment and not his son. 

 

Now, he beamed, telling all of Oscar’s aunts and uncles about his promotion. Wearing a McLaren shirt that Oscar got him for Christmas proudly.  

 

“Working for the reigning champ,” his dad said at dinner earlier, voice full of reverence. “That must be nice. I bet it’s all private jets and hotel suites from here on out.” 

 

Hardly. Oscar still flew economy with the rest of the traveling team while Lando and Daniel lounged in private planes with Zak and whatever dickhead sponsor wanted facetime with them. 

 

Oscar stayed in a reasonable hotel room. While Lando got a suite with his special mattress.

 

Oscar got cheap takeaway with the mechanics while Lando dined at expensive restaurants with other drivers.

 

Though he did get his own room now. Something he knew was a bit of a pitwall perk. 

 

“Oh, it must be nice to work with Lando,” his dad’s girlfriend had gushed. “Surely you get to meet celebrities all the time. I saw Beyoncé was at a race.” 

 

From the corner of his eye, he could see his sister rolling her eyes. 

 

“I don’t get much time to mingle,” Oscar confessed. “A lot of prep work before the races and stuff.” 

 

“Of course,” his dad said. “Shame you couldn’t be with Riccardo this year, though. With that rocketship you have, well, might be the first Australian Champion since Webber.” 

 

“Mark Webber never won a championship,” Oscar said, picking at the label of his beer bottle. Oscar tried not to flinch at the mention of him. Mark Webber, who promised to get Oscar into F1 and then dropped him the second he couldn’t drive anymore. He wonders if Mark even remembers his name anymore or if he’s moved into his next young pet project. 

 

“He didn’t?’ his dad said, looking stumped. “We’re in desperate need of an Aussie champ then.” 

 

“I think we have to cheer for Oscar’s side of the garage,” one of his sisters interrupted, laughing. “Blood before country, surely?” 

 

“Of course,” his dad said, nodding. “Norris it is, then. Even if he is a bit soft.”

 

Oscar somewhat agreed, but he hated hearing it from his dad’s mouth. 

 

“Plus, Lando Norris is hot as fuck,” his sister added, making Oscar’s nose wrinkle. He didn’t need to know who his sister thought was attractive. Even if she wasn’t totally wrong. 

 

He’d be a complete idiot not to notice that Lando is good-looking. Infuriating so. It’s bad enough when he rolls up to the paddock in some posh, fuckboy outfit and carefully styled curls. But it’s worse when he’s wandering hospitality after a session in the car, sweat drying on his skin and face flushed. He still looks good. It makes Oscar ticked off in ways he can’t even fully articulate. 

 

He sighs and falls onto his back. It’s still warm here. Usually, he welcomes it after the chill that seems to hang everywhere in England, but now it feels stifling. He shoves the blankets down. 

 

It would be easy to blame the inability to sleep on the jetlag and the anxiety of the new season. 

 

Rather, it’s Lando’s words that have him tossing and turning. 

 

For a long time, Oscar has accepted that he’s never been the most emotive. That his voice is flat and borders on emotionless. Comments from teachers and friends and his family. All wondering if he’s enjoying himself. Or if he’s affected by things. He thinks about sitting at the kitchen table on a rare break from school as his parents broke the news of their divorce. 

 

His sisters had burst into tears immediately. 

 

And Oscar had felt devastated. 

 

Like everything he knew and loved was being broken into two. That life as he knew it was changing irretrievably. Even more than it already had after he left for England.

 

 Like, somehow it was his fault. 

 

“How do you feel, Osc?” his mum had asked, coming to cradle his face in her hands. Her eyes were sad and wet with unshed tears as she searched his eyes. “Oh, my brave boy. All strong for his sisters.”

 

Oscar hadn’t felt strong. He hadn’t even meant to keep his face so stoic. He’d honestly felt like his entire being had been cracked open. Surely, that reflected on his face. In his slowly tearing eyes. 

 

But it hadn’t. And even as a child, he couldn’t help but preen at being called strong.

 

Because that was the truth, wasn’t it? Strength was not letting your emotions get the better of you. Of not showing them. Showing emotion was showing weakness. One, not becoming of a boy who aimed to be a man in Formula 1. 

 

Even as fate put him on the sidelines and he watched Lando stand atop his car, eyes rimmed red with tears and mouth open in an endless stream of love and gratitude for everyone around him. 

 

It was confusing. Still is. 

 

Oscar’s strong. He doesn’t make his problems anyone else’s burden. Even when his own dreams slipped through his fingers, he dusted off his sadness and picked himself up again. Got an education and made a new future for himself. 

 

Yet, Lando gets to live the dream Oscar’s always wanted. While being emotional. 

 

Soft.

 

Weak. 

 

The word hangs around the back of his mind like a cloying, sinister fog. 

 

Oscar was strong and got a consolation prize as a life. 

 

Lando was weak and got everything he’s ever wanted. 

 

It makes everything Oscar’s known flip on its head. 

 

And the worst part is, he can’t help but think it’s too late. He can’t be the positive light in Lando’s ear. Can’t be as effortlessly upbeat as him. Can’t express himself so freely and openly. 

 

He sighs and rolls onto his side, burrowing his cheek into the pillow. 

 

He can’t do this. 

 

He’s going to get fired. Lose another precious thing in his life. 

 

Because he can't please Lando Norris. 

 

He squeezes his eyes shut. 

 

Fuck.

 

He has to please Lando Norris. 

 

Or he loses everything. 

 

----

 

It’s a wet day at the track. Rain soaks through his team polo and sets a light chill on his skin before Oscar even reaches hospitality. 

 

Oscar likes Albert Park. Loves it actually. He grew up here. He’s seen cars race here. He spent most of his formative years wishing to race here. To be the first Australian winner here. 

 

But being here, drenched in rain, and feeling the weight of the impending season on his shoulders, makes him feel less than nostalgic at the moment. 

 

“All good, mate?” Will asks, looking at Oscar, who clutches a coffee cup in his hand while he waits for someone to bring him an extra shirt and a new jacket.  

 

“Ah, yeah,” Oscar says, awkwardly. “Just you know, Lando’s on pole and everything. Rain. A lot of pressure.” 

 

Will laughs. “Sure, but the best place for the champ to be. Besides, he knows what he’s got to do.” 

 

Sure, he does. But does Oscar? Before the weekend, he’d promised himself that he would try what Lando wanted. That he would give some encouragement. He would try to listen. Or at least acknowledge that he was listening.

 

He’s sure he’s run all three of his sisters’ ears off trying to figure out how he could be better. For the team. And for Lando. 

 

“Why don’t you just write down some general encouraging words beforehand?” his sister suggested when they’d gone to the beach on Wednesday. “If you’re stuck, just pull something from there. Or I’m sure Lando would just take a good job.” 

 

“Is it that easy?” he asked. 

 

She’d laughed, high and delighted. “Yes, it is. Most people are simple creatures. They just wanna know they’re not fuckups. Not everything is an issue to be solved.” 

 

It sounds so simple. But it sits in Oscar’s sternum like a badly swallowed pill. 

 

It’s not that he can’t give praise or a compliment where it’s needed. Or that he doesn’t think Lando can do a good job. 

 

He’s the world champion for a reason. 

 

It just doesn’t come naturally to him. Not when he’s elbow deep in data and strategy. He has other things on his mind. Different things for the driver to try. Keeping track of the delta and pace. Communicating with the pit crew and the rest of the pit wall as needed. Keeping an ear open for what the other teams are doing. 

 

It hadn’t even really come naturally as a driver. Not when he’s felt like every race was a fight for his future. He needed the information, not mindless praise. He knew if he was doing a good job when he was in the lead. Everything else was just a chance to do better.

 

But Lando isn’t like that. He needs more. He needs someone in his ear who understands him. 

 

And Oscar… just doesn’t. 

 

He sighs into his coffee. 

 

“With Daniel in P2, it certainly makes my job easier,” Will says, with an easy laugh. “Well, unless they bang into each other. Or we have a team rivalry on our hands.” 

 

“What?” Oscar asks. “Surely, we’ll give Lando priority as the champion?” 

 

Will coughs and looks away. He rubs at the back of his neck. “We’ll keep the same rules as last year.” 

 

“But, Andrea and Zak clearly want Lando to go back to back,” Oscar says. “And you must too,” 

 

“Ah, it’s not my decision,” Will shrugs. “Zak and Andrea have been meeting. The priority will be the team. And they’re ok to let Lando and Daniel race as long as it doesn’t compromise our points as a team.”

 

“Why haven’t I been told this?” Oscar asks, feeling a bit left out and angry. “So, what? We won’t be doing team orders?” 

 

“Well, we shouldn't even need them today,” Will tries. “Lando’s on pole and he’s faster. You’ll be fine, Oscar.”

 

“Lando knows about this?” 

 

Will nods. “Zak was very clear and spoke to him directly this morning. They just finalized their decision. I was going to tell you and Tom later, but… here we are.” 

 

Oscar exhales loudly through his nose. He’s irritated. He never expected the team to become like Red Bull, putting all their eggs in one basket. But he expected some preferential treatment for Lando. Nothing insane, but surely they would make sure Lando has the best chances in these races. That their reigning champion would get preference. 

 

There have been rumors of Daniel being dropped ever since Oscar came onto the team full-time. 

 

Now they have a championship-contending car for the second time and a reigning world champion, and they want to give Daniel a chance?

 

He grits his teeth. 

 

“Alright,” Oscar says. “Thanks for telling me.” 

 

Will offers him a kind but firm smile. “He doesn’t need team orders. Lando can do this on his own.” 

 

Sure. 

 

But can Oscar? 

----

 

It’s a shit race from the start. 

 

Rain. Delayed start. Cars retiring before they even really go racing. Max’s car practically up his ass. 

 

Not to mention, the hint of papaya behind Max, bright and glaring in Lando’s mirrors. 

 

Hard enough to try to prove himself as the reigning world champion. Now he’s got a three-time champion and a home race hero behind him. 

 

Lando loves Australia, but he could do without a certain Australian. 

 

Make that two, actually. 

 

“Lando, we’re looking at rain potentially in 10 minutes,” Oscar drones in his ear, as flat and impassive as usual. “Class 1. Probably one or two laps. And then maybe increasing.” 

 

Lando sighs into his helmet. He looks ahead, focuses on the dry line. Tries to ignore the ghost of Max coming closer and closer to him. 

 

“Where’s Daniel?” Lando asks. 

 

“.5 to Verstappen,” Oscar says. “Focus on staying on track.” 

 

Piss off, Lando thinks, but says nothing. Focus on the words, not the tone.

 

That’s what he promised. Still, even if he just takes the words and imagines them in a bright, joyful tone, they still piss him off. 

 

“You’re currently the fastest car on track,” Oscar says. “Shit.”

 

“What?” Lando says. “What happened?”

 

Lando answers his own question. He sees a flash of orange much closer than it used to be. Daniel’s passed Max. 

 

“Verstappen slid,” Oscar says. “Make sure you’re staying on the dry line. Daniel, now 2.4 behind.” 

 

Lando grits his teeth. 

 

Drops are now starting to hit his visor. He grips the wheel tighter. 

 

He can’t lose it. Can’t lose this race. 

 

His therapist would be so disappointed to know he’s even kinda thinking of what people online would say. 

 

Forgettable Champion. 

 

McLaren Golden Boy.

 

He thinks of how deafening the cheers will be if an Australian wins. How no matter what Lando does, he would never live down losing like this. 

 

Villain, if he takes the win away from an Aussie at home. 

 

Villain, if he can’t prove his worth as a champion here and now in the rain. 

 

The car slips a bit underneath him, sliding slightly on a wet, painted line. 

 

“How far is he now?” 

 

“2.3,” Oscar says easily before pausing. 

 

Lando thinks the radio has fully turned off, but there’s still a light hum in the background. The faint sound of others talking. 

 

“Focus on your race, Lando,” Oscar says, tightly. “You’re doing a good job.” 

 

Oh.

 

Lando blinks. 

 

The words are so simple. Anyone could say that. But in Oscar’s voice. They sound almost timid. Like he’s speaking a new language for the first time. 

 

Lando readjusts his hands on the wheel. His gloves are getting wet from the rain. 

 

He can do this. 

 

“Thank you, Oscar.” 

----

 

Oscar’s eyes dart between Lando and Daniel’s times. 

 

The gap is closing. 

 

Will and Andrea are talking softly to the side of him. He watches Lando’s onboard. Lando is coming up to traffic. 

 

.6 gap. 

 

Fuck, Oscar thinks. 

 

He racks his brain for something. Anything to push Lando. Push his car forward. 

 

Daniel’s had the fastest time for the last few laps. 

 

Get the fuck out of the way, Ocon. 

 

“How are the tyres?” Oscar asks. 

 

“Fading,” Lando replies, almost immediately, clearly out of breath. 

 

Oscar looks over to Tom. He’s hunched over his screens, rubbing at his brow. He glances at Oscar. Glances at the rest of the wall. 

 

“Daniel is faster,” he says. “We should invert positions. Daniel is asking.” 

 

Of course, he fucking is. 

 

1.1 gap. 

 

“No,” Oscar says. “Lando’s in the lead, and there’s no one else we’re fighting.” 

 

“How much faster?” Andrea asks.

 

“Does it matter?” Oscar argues. “If he wants track position, he should have qualified for it.” 

 

“He’s been quicker by a tenth for the last 10 laps,” Tom says.

 

“Then he can fight for it,” Oscar says. He looks at Will and Andrea. Look at Zak, whose normally overly expressive face is set in a hard line. “That’s what we agreed to, right? That we would let them race.” 

 

Andrea says nothing for a moment. A long moment. Oscar taps his fingers impatiently across the desk. 

 

“Too dangerous in the wet,” he says, finally. “They can race when we go on slicks.”

 

Tom nods and relays the message to Daniel. 

 

Oscar sighs. 

 

.8 gap. 

----

 

Fuck these safety cars. 

 

Not actually, Lando thinks, hoping and waiting for Oscar’s confirmation that Fernando’s ok. 

 

But the last thing he needs is for Max to get back in the mix with Daniel. 

 

“Fernando is ok,” Oscar says after a long moment. “We’ll box this lap for hards. This will put Max back behind Daniel if he pits.”

 

Ok. Lando takes a deep breath.

 

If those two wanna race. 

 

Then Lando’s ready to fucking race. 

 

----

 

Oscar looks at the weather map. 

 

It’s going to rain again. 

 

He bites his lip. 

 

“How certain are we that it might only be for a lap?” Lando asks. 

 

Oscar sighs before he presses the radio button. He grits his teeth. “Uncertain. Just based on everything that’s happened today.” 

 

The skies are so dark and gray, it might as well be night. The air in the pit lane picks up in a cold, wet chill. 

 

Slicks. 

 

Inters. 

 

He looks at the rest of the wall. There’s debating around him. 

 

Oscar hates rain. Hates that there are mounds of technology to track weather, and it’s still completely unpredictable. 

 

“It’s very dark,” Lando says pointedly, voice starting to go a bit high. “Surely, we should put the inters on now, rather than spin out.” 

 

He can hear the anxiety in Lando’s voice. It’s subtle and hidden in some of the crackles of the radio, but it’s there. 

 

He’s sure Lando’s thinking of Sochi. Of every other wet race that hasn’t gone his way. 

 

If they have the wrong tyre, either way, they’d be fucked. Oscar looks over his screens. Rain is coming. But how fast? Will it fall here? 

 

They should wait. 

 

Until they know. Until it’s certain. 

 

Right?

 

“Checking,” Oscar says, feeling far younger and unsure than he should, sitting here. Zak puts a hand out into the pit lane, feeling a thick drop of rain on his hand. 

 

He watches as the safety car peels in. 

 

As Lando dashes off on the restart. 

 

As Lando, then Daniel, run off into the gravel. 

 

As Daniel’s car slips and slides off into the grass.

 

“We need to box,” Lando says. “Now.” 

 

Shit. 

 

Lando’s right as much as Oscar hates to admit. 

 

He doesn’t hesitate. He puts the call into the pit crew. 

 

“Yes, box box,” Oscar says as a flurry of activity shuffles around him. The pit crew is getting the tyres ready. Andrea is speaking rapidly to someone. 

 

Max taking the lead. 

 

Tom talking quickly into his headset, trying to get Daniel out of the grass. 

 

“Did he stop?” Lando asks, breath coming in heavy pants. 

 

“No,” Oscar says. “Focus on keeping the car on track. You can do this, Lando.” 

 

Lando’s going to win this race. 

 

Oscar’s never felt more sure of anything. 

----

 

Another safety car. 

 

“We should stay on the inters,” Lando says, praying and hoping Oscar will listen to him. 

 

Oscar is silent for a moment. “Confirm. Don’t come in. We’ll stay on these until the end.” 

 

Lando takes a deep breath. 

 

“Max is going to be close,” Oscar says. “Keep it clean.” 

 

“What the fuck else would I do?” Lando says on instinct without any heat. He can’t see it, but he imagines that Oscar’s rolling his eyes at him. 

 

“It’s my job to make sure you don’t run the car into the wall,” Oscar says as dry and droll as ever. “Unless you fancy being the cause of a fourth safety car.”

 

“Yeah, yeah, fuck off,” Lando says, grinning under the helmet. His stomach is twisting in nerves. His body feels rigid with anxious energy. He looks behind. Max is swerving and warming his tyres. But he feels as good as ever. 

 

Miami good. 

 

World Champion good. 

 

“If there’s another safety car, what slicks would you prefer?” Oscar asks. 

 

“Oh, you’re asking my opinion now?” Lando asks, watching the back of the safety car intensely. 

 

“Lando,” Oscar warns. 

 

“Put the other tyres away,” Lando says. “We won’t need them.” 

 

“I’ll have the mediums ready in case.”

 

“Never listen, do you, Piastri?” Lando says, but he feels he hardly means it. “I can do it on the inters. I’ve got this.” 

 

Oscar is silent for a moment. 

 

“Alright, go win us a race then,” Oscar says. “Just keep it clean, mate. No need to push. Just bring it over the line.”

 

“Copy.”

 

“Safety car in this lap,” Oscar says. He pauses, the static sounding in Lando’s ear as Oscar takes a deep, steady breath over the line. 

 

“You’re doing so good, Lando.” 

 

Ah. 

 

The words send an unexpected jolt up Lando’s body. Something sharp and electric up his spine, that settles warm and dizzy in his brain and bones. 

 

He blinks to steady his vision. To keep the car in the dry line of the track. 

 

Fucking hell, he thinks. 

 

Oscar thinks he’s good. 

 

Lando swallows, breathing harshly into his balaclava. 

 

He watches as the safety car peels back in the pits. He checks his mirrors. Max is close. Waiting for the moment, Lando fucks the start. 

 

Well, that won’t be happening today, Lando decides. 

 

----

 

Lando is resplendent standing on the podium. 

 

His eyes are closed as he tilts his face up towards the sun that had hidden itself away for most of the day. Coming out only to shine down upon today’s victor. 

 

Oscar watches with the rest of the team. 

 

The British national anthem does little for Oscar normally, but watching Lando inspires some sort of quasi-national pride within him. 

 

He’s almost…beautiful, Oscar thinks. Watching as Lando soaks in every moment of this win. 

 

His curls are wet with sweat and rain.  His lips split into a bright, delighted smile. His body lax with a sort of unspeakable relief. 

 

The sight of it inspires a sort of awe that scratches at Oscar’s brain and beats at his chest. 

 

He looks away. Focuses on the side where Max is standing, somewhat annoyed and irritated at another loss. Drags his gaze to the TV crew in front of him, trying to get a good shot of Lando’s mum. Looking around at the sea of papaya shirts around him. 

 

His eyes can’t help but drift back to Lando. 

 

Pretty when he wins, Oscar thinks before he can stop himself, or the way his breath catches in his throat at the sight. The thought is so strong and startling it almost makes Oscar lightheaded. 

 

Yet, it rings so true and real in Oscar’s head. 

 

I’m losing my fucking mind working with him, Oscar thinks, watching as Lando accepts his award. He looks at the metal so proud and fond, it makes Oscar’s chest feel tight. Get back to work.

 

He’s gone the second Lando does his signature champagne pop. 

 

----

 

Lando finds him hiding in hospitality, where Oscar’s fiddling around with his laptop. 

 

“Hey.” 

 

“Hey,” Oscar says, offering a tight smile. “Congrats, mate.” 

 

“Thanks,” Lando says. “Guess the teambuilding worked a bit then.” 

 

Oscar shrugs. “I guess so.” 

 

Lando laughs a bit and tugs at where his race suit is tied around his waist. 

 

Oscar averts his eyes, definitely not taking note of Lando’s large, veiny hands in comparison to the drastic, slim slope of his waist. 

 

“So, car works then?” Oscar says. “Guess, I was right during testing.” 

 

Lando rolls his eyes. “I have notes.” 

 

“You’re joking,” Oscar says. “You won the race, Lando.” 

 

“Wet race though,” Lando says, shrugging. “Not a good picture for the whole thing.” 

 

“If anything, this proves how good the car is in difficult conditions.” 

 

“Hm, the car? Or the driver?” 

 

“The car.” 

 

“I heard on the radio that I did a good job,” Lando says.

 

“You did,” Oscar says flatly. “So did the car. And the engineers and the mechanics. Everyone who worked on it did a good job.” 

 

“Yeah, but today,” Lando starts. “It was the driver who got it over the line.” 

 

“If that’s what you want to believe,” Oscar scoffs, even if it’s true. 

 

“Well, I distinctly remember someone taking my advice and it working,” Lando says, lightly. A smug sort of smirk on his lips.

 

“I don’t remember that,” Oscar says, standing. He closes his laptop and rests his hands on the table. 

 

“I guess I’ll just have to keep proving myself right then,” Lando says. “Seems like we can win races when you listen to me.” 

 

“Whatever you say, Norris.” Oscar rolls his eyes, watching as Lando’s mouth fits into a shit-eating grin. Such a warm, friendly expression aimed right at Oscar. 

 

Someone in the distance is walking around, collecting people for a team photo. Oscar smooths out his team polo. He needs to get out of here. 

 

“I’m about to prove you wrong so fucking hard, Piastri,” Lando says, voice low and full of confidence only someone who won today could have. The words send equal parts thrill and irritation through his body. 

 

“If it helps you to believe that,” Oscar says, watching as a sort of delirious delight overtake Lando’s face. He’s smiling so wide, Oscar can see the little gap between his front teeth. 

 

“Hm, you’ll see,” Lando says. “Osc.”

 

Right. Oscar needs to go.

 

He makes a thin excuse and leaves before Lando can see just how deeply the flush on his cheeks can get. 

 

Because honestly? 

 

If seeing Lando this happy is what he means by emotion, then maybe Oscar gets it a bit. He thinks he might do a lot to keep his driver smiling like that weekend after weekend. 




Chapter End Notes

~~Ohhhh, they're noticing each other~~ Thank you so much for taking the time to read! Everyone's feedback and love are much appreciated and loved! I fear these chapters just get longer and longer, but they're fun to write, so the time passes quite quickly lol. Bless my F1TV subscription for allowing me to rewatch Australia 2025 cause I forgot how wild that race was before Oscar even slid into the grass.

Also, our boys got a podium together! finalllyyy! I just need Lando to get a win as a champion, and I will be happier than ever.

Chapter 4: Miami Miami Miami

Chapter Summary

Back to square one. A friendly jog. A much needed chat. Lando's version of doing something nice.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Australia seems to be where Lando’s luck runs out. 

 

As does both of their patience and any goodwill they’d shared from his win. 

 

In China, Oscar struggles to help Lando find pace the entire sprint and watches him nurse his brake issue onto the podium as Daniel gets pole and wins.

 

In Japan, Lando fights Max for the win, but ultimately loses. 

 

In Bahrain, Lando continues to struggle with the car. Qualifying behind Daniel and scraping by on the podium. 

 

Not to mention the penalty. Something that makes Oscar want to shake Lando by the shoulders for something so basic and stupid. Even worse, when Lando comes up to him after the race, clearly upset and still brimming with anger. 

 

“You hardly helped me at all,” Lando says, lips so pushed together they may as well be a pout. “Big ass brain and it does nothing.” 

 

If Oscar didn’t know he was filled with unnecessary emotion, he might even have the audacity to be offended. 

 

“I did everything I could on my end,” Oscar bristles. “We got you back out into the chase. Got you back on the podium. You’re the only one who made a rookie mistake.” 

 

“I was trying to give myself the best start,” Lando argues. “It’s a guess after a while. When I can’t see the line anymore.” 

 

“Then don’t guess,” Oscar says, pointedly. “You’re clearly not good at it.” 

 

That one makes Lando roll his eyes and scoff like a child. 

 

“Fuck off, Oscar,” Lando says, seething. “You know how this car is. I can’t fucking get it to work for me. I have to try and make the best start I can.”

 

This has been a constant topic of conversation between them. In meetings. In emails. In Teams messages. 

 

Oscar didn’t even know the drivers had access to Teams. Between his day-to-day work and the constant pings from Lando, the ding of a message notification is now becoming a haunting sound for him. 

 

They’d spent hours poring over this perfect, fast car. That somehow was Lando’s new enemy. Oscar has enough sense not to point out how it seems to be working for the other side of the garage. Despite Daniel’s slide off in Melbourne, he was thriving in the car. Taking poles and wins. Driving it to the limit. Driving better than Lando. 

 

Performing better than he ever had with Oscar in his ear. 

 

It would be a lie to say it didn’t sting Oscar a bit. To watch as Daniel took victory after victory with Tom. Meanwhile, Oscar has a little brat in his headphones, moaning and complaining race after race. 

 

Beyond the brake issue in China, the car was perfect. It’s not the car that’s the issue. It’s Lando.

 

“I’m trying my best,” Lando says, huffing. He crosses his arms, and Oscar pointedly avoids the sight of his Nomex stretching over his thick biceps. “I’ve told you before. It’s a good car, but it doesn't suit my driving style.”

 

“Then why are you only having issues?” Oscar asks. He leans his hip against the desk in the garage, fixing Lando with a hard stare. 

 

“Is your inability to actually think outside your own box an engineer thing or an Oscar thing?” Lando asks. “I can’t push this car. I can’t predict what it will do. It’s difficult for me.” 

 

‘It’s not like you’re last and Daniel’s first, Lando,” Oscar says. “You’ve been on the podium every time.” 

 

“If the car were more suited to me, I could be winning every time,” Lando asserts. “Not trying to be a cocky asshole or anything, but c’mon.” 

 

“You c’mon,” Oscar says. “Are you a professional, experienced driver or not? If yes, then you should be able to adjust to the car.” 

 

Lando eyes him, those normally wide, expressive eyes, set in hard, dark lines. 

 

“Is your hairline so bad because your brain fucking cooked it off?” 

 

“What the fuck?” Oscar asks, hands twitching at his side, to come up and push his hair down across his forehead. “It’s a widow’s peak.” 

 

“Sure,” Lando says, laughing smugly to himself. “Keep telling yourself that.” 

 

“Lando,” Oscar tries, scrubbing a hand down his face. “We’re not doing any upgrades right now. So stop with the childish insults and get back to defending your title. Are you the champion or not?”

 

“I am,” Lando says petulantly. 

 

“Then act like it.” 

 

Lando has nothing left to say after that, storming off with his 3rd place cap and his lips set into a true pout. 

 

A few days later, Oscar gets an email. 

 

I want grid tape.

 

That’s all it says. And it takes everything in Oscar not to send back something petty. To call him a rookie for needing fucking grid tape to make sure he doesn’t miss his grid box. To get a roll of tape and just slap a piece right over Lando’s big mouth. 

 

The worst of it comes in Saudi Arabia. 

 

It’s hot. Hotter than Bahrain. And their days are late. Far later than Oscar would like. 

 

He sighs into his coffee, watching as the clock ticks down for Q3. 

 

“Ok, Lando,” Oscar says, doing his best to keep his voice even and neutral. “Just keep doing what you did in Q2. We don’t need to fight the car the whole way. You don’t have to attack the corners as much.”

 

“Copy,” Lando says. “Don’t do anything I know. Hope and pray I figure it out.”

 

His voice is nearly acidic in Oscar’s ears. Dripping in bitterness and dissatisfaction. With the car. With Oscar. 

 

He twists around in his seat and watches as Lando tears out of the garage. As he does his outlap. Oscar looks up, eyes flitting to the bright lights of the garage. He prays to whatever entity is out there that Lando can do well. That the car and the driver have some sort of click. That it gets better. 

 

In a becoming fashion for both of them, their prayers fall on deaf ears. 

 

Instead, Oscar watches, his stomach twisting in sharp spikes of anxiety, as Lando hits the kerb and spins. He swallows harshly as Lando’s car hits the barriers and the red flag comes out. 

 

The other nine cars tumble back into the pits as Oscar hits the radio button.

 

“Are you ok?” 

 

There’s silence for a long moment. He can see Lando’s head moving a bit from the overhead shot, but the time before he speaks feels like forever. 

 

“Yeah, I’m ok,” Lando says. His breathing is heavy and a bit pained, like the hit to the wall took a lot out of him. 

 

But he’s speaking, and that’s enough for Oscar, even if it means they’ll line up 10th on the grid tomorrow. He’s about to respond, find the perfect time for Lando to hop out when he speaks again. 

 

“Fucking idiot.”

 

The words are hushed, clearly meant for himself and not Oscar. He’s not even sure Lando knows that the radio is still. 

 

Maybe Oscar would agree. Should agree. 

 

But the tone of the words, laden with disappointment and self-loathing, hit Oscar hard in the chest. 

 

It’s one thing to have Lando mad at him. To bitch at him and nitpick and think he’s a prick. It’s another to listen as Lando’s breathing comes in heavy pants and know he’s hating himself. That he’s upset with no one but himself. 

 

That the inability to adjust is sitting so heavily on Lando’s shoulders. 

 

That this man, this champion, who’s always excelled on Saturdays, is struggling. Is being forced to pay for his own discomfort in the car every Sunday. 

 

It strikes Oscar violently that there is nothing he can do in this moment. That he can look over his data again and again. But he can’t teach Lando how to drive. How to get comfortable in this car. 

 

It leaves him feeling open and unsure. As much as it fills him with anger. That he’s doing everything right, and it doesn’t matter if Lando can’t make it work. 

 

That maybe, Lando’s doing everything he can, and it still isn’t working. 

 

“It’s ok,” Oscar says, evenly over the radio. “You’re free to get out of the car now. Medical car coming as well.” 

 

“Oh,” Lando says, like he wasn’t expecting to hear Oscar. “Right. Ok. Thanks, mate.” 

 

Once Lando gets back to the garage, Oscar knows he's in for a fight. 

 

“What the fuck?” Lando asks. “What the fuck is wrong with this fucking car?” 

 

“Lando, I told you,” Oscar says.

 

“Don’t say it,” Lando says, holding his hand up. “I know there’s nothing wrong with it on your stupid little charts and Excel sheets. But I’m telling you it’s shit. It goes against everything I know.” 

 

“What do you want me to do, Lando?” 

 

“I want you to do your actual job,” he says. “Give me things to help. You want me to go against everything I know. And not push these cars to the limit.” 

 

“We’re not in the midfield anymore, Lando,” Oscar argues. “We don’t have to push these to the limit.” 

 

“Right,” Lando scoffs. “My opinion matters when we can’t even get out of Q1, but suddenly it means nothing when there’s a bit of pace. When my teammate can drive it with ease.” 

 

“I didn’t say that.” 

 

“But it's the truth,” Lando says. “I changed my entire driving style and all my preferences to help bring a shitbox onto the podium. But suddenly, when we’re at the top, we can’t build a car I prefer? No, I have to adjust all over again. No wonder Max will never leave redbull. At least, they know how to treat their fucking champion.” 

 

“Lando,” Oscar starts. 

 

“No,” Lando says. “It’s fucking true. You think Mercedes or Redbull would let the worse teammate race against their fucking reigning champion? It’s bullshit.” 

 

In some ways, Oscar agrees. Can’t help, but not when his job is to get said champion wins. But he has a feeling, he might think differently if he were still on the other side. 

 

“Do you even want that?” Oscar asks, feeling like even Lando thinks differently.

 

“Of course not,” Lando says, instantly. “But we can at least bring my car something to make it better for me. I don’t care what they do for Daniel, but you’re my race engineer, and you should be fighting for upgrades that make me more comfortable. That let me do what I do best.” 

 

“I can’t force upgrades before we’re ready,” Oscar tries. 

 

“Then what are you good for?” 

 

“We can try some different things on the car,” Oscar says. “Until the upgrades. But you have to learn to adjust.” 

 

“Is it your personal aim to piss me off every weekend?” Lando asks, ruffling a hand through his hair and narrowing his eyes at Oscar. 

 

It’s not. It’s his goal to do the opposite. To make sure that Lando succeeds. That he stands on the top step. That he knows it’s Oscar’s words and help that put him there.

 

He’s just failing miserably at it. 

 

“Is it your personal mission to be a brat every chance you get?” Oscar asks, voice dripping with derision.

 

“Every time I think I’ve seen how much of a dick you can be, you go and prove me wrong,” Lando sneers. 

 

“Christ, you’re so -,” Oscar starts before he’s cut off.

 

“Boys.” 

 

They both turn to see Andrea looking at them. His brow raised and his mouth set in a firm, unhappy line. His hands rest on his hips.

 

He shakes his head. 

 

It makes Oscar feel like a scolded child. 

 

Like, he shouldn’t be here in the first place. 

 

----

 

There’s very few things Lando wants to do after Saudi other than lie on his couch and mope. Or spend hours bothering Max over a game. 

 

Little he wants to do when he’s lost the championship lead to his teammate. 

 

He’s already deleted Instagram and Twitter from his phone, less than interested in hearing what the rest of the world thinks of him. 

 

That he’s a failure. A terrible champion. That the team should start favoring Daniel. 

 

That he’s weak. 

 

Maybe he is weak. 

 

He’s certainly tired. 

 

There was a confidence and an ease that came with winning the championship. Seeing the 1 on his car and being called the reigning champion everywhere. 

 

But the pressure didn’t seem to go away. 

 

And the desire to win. 

 

It felt worse than ever. Clawing up his throat and threatening to beat out of his chest. He had a taste for winning, and he never wanted to let go of it. 

 

Australia had been good. An earned victory. 

 

But every moment after that felt like he was dragging himself across the finish line. Sure, he was still getting podiums. Still in the top 5 most of the time. But the car. 

 

Every time he got in it, it felt like he was lost. Like he’d never driven a Formula 1 car before. His hands shook at the wheel. His anger lit up like a match when he couldn't improve his times. His frustration boiled over in terse messages to Oscar. 

 

The tentative olive branch they’d extended in Melbourne had rotted. 

 

And now, Lando has to be here. Tying his shoes up and waiting for Oscar outside the MTC. 

 

For a jog. 

 

He should be in Monaco, feeling depressed, along the Mediterranean coast. Should be bumming yacht days off other drivers. Should be drowning in self-pity and surface-level conversations with whichever Instagram model slinked his way.  

 

Not getting ready to run with Oscar under dull English skies. 

 

Apparently, this is what he does in his free time. Freak. 

 

Though Jon and his therapist agreed that the run would be good for him. And his relationship with Oscar

 

“Hey.”

 

Lando looks up.

 

It’s like padel all over again. Oscar comes out of the factory doors, wearing shorts and a tight shirt. Tiny little running shorts that show off the corded muscles of his legs. Lean except for his thick, muscular thighs that have Lando’s mouth running dry. 

 

And the shirt is frankly obscene for having long sleeves. They cling to all the bulk and dips of Oscar's arms and torso. His back and shoulders, so big and broad, make Lando squirm. 

 

He offers Lando a soft smile, barely more than a slight uptick of his lips. 

 

“Hi Oscar,” Lando says glumly. He shoves his hands into his pockets. It’s almost May, but it’s still a tinge too cold for him. 

 

“Ready to go?” Oscar asks, fiddling with his watch. 

 

“Guess so,” Lando says with a shrug. 

 

“You ok to do a 5K?” Oscar asks, looking at him. 

 

“Jesus,” Lando says. It’s nothing he can’t do. But in his free time?  He grimaces. “Yeah, I suppose so.” 

 

They take off in silence, running side by side down the long drive of the MTC. The late afternoon sun peeks through a blanket of light clouds and the trees lining the road. The sound of their shoes pounding against the pavement and their synced breath feels loud to Lando’s ears. 

 

They stay silent as they turn out of the entrance gate, nodding to the security guard as they go. Oscar strides next to Lando easily. He knows Oscar keeps up with the exercise. 

 

Has to with that fucking insane body. 

 

But he keeps Lando’s pace like it’s nothing. 

 

“Where are we going?” Lando asks after a moment, hating how out of breath he sounds already. He knows they’re heading back towards the center of town. 

 

“My flat isn’t far,” Oscar says. “I figured we could chat there afterwards.” 

 

That sounds ominous. Lando isn’t quite sure what they have to chat about when he’s said everything he has to say. 

 

Unless it’s some ruse for Oscar to fucking murder him. 

 

“Chat about what?” 

 

“Us,” Oscar huffs. “We’re clearly not working.”  

 

“That we can agree on,” Lando says, rolling his eyes. 

 

“Then let’s find another way to make it work,” Oscar says. He says it so easily, like he isn’t half of the issue. “I just want to talk for a bit. I won’t hold you captive.”

 

“Yeah, fine,” Lando says, dropping behind Oscar. He’s tired. He doesn’t want to be here. 

 

“I have, uh, green smoothies and stuff,” Oscar offers. 

 

Lando grunts. He narrows his eyes at the broad expanse of Oscar’s back. Watching as his muscles shift with each step he takes. 

 

“Mint,” Lando says absently, soaking in the line of Oscar’s shoulders. The swing of his biceps. The tight cords of his calves. “Let’s not talk the rest of the run.” 

 

Oscar doesn’t say anything back, but Lando can see his jaw tightening in response. 

 

Prick, Lando thinks. 

 

-----

 

They both pant, trying to catch their breath as they stumble over Oscar’s entryway. 

 

“Shit, you go hard, Pastry,” Lando says, half bent over.

 

“I don’t like that one very much,” Oscar says, toeing off his shoes and walking towards his kitchen. He opens the fridge and grabs two water bottles, tossing one to Lando once he follows him. 

 

“Oh, you prefer Osc?” Lando asks. “Or does dickhead really do it for you?” 

 

“Sure,” he says. He fixes Lando with a blank, focused stare. “You did really well today, Lando. Good pace.”

 

Lando chokes a bit on the water in his mouth. 

 

“Oh god,” Oscar says, worrying he’s overstepped his boundaries. “Are you ok?” 

 

Lando nods as he coughs out the water stuck in his throat. 

 

“Yeah,” Lando rasps. “Just, uh, didn’t expect that from you. I mean, I’m hardly a runner.” 

 

“You manage,” Oscar says. “I didn’t use to be either, but it’s a good way to ease my mind after a long day or weekend.” 

 

“Well, it’s certainly less torture than letting Jon train my neck,” Lando agrees. 

 

“Yeah, I do not fucking miss that,” Oscar says, laughing. His eyes flit up to Lando, taking in the heft of his neck. The way his shirt collar is stretched around the pure girth of his muscled, trained neck. Just a hint of the strength Lando holds. So at odds with the lean nature of his body, pure muscle hiding beneath the facade of a slim, tapered waist and a chisled, delicate face. 

 

Masculine, no doubt. But the type of masculinity that’s immortalized in marble and paint. Something to be seen in a museum. Something to be looked at, but not touched. 

 

Oscar scratches unconsciously at his own neck, thin and pale. He swallows and looks around the kitchen. Feeling uncertain. Feeling a bit out of control. 

 

“Do you run during race weekends?” Lando asks. He’s gotten his breath back and is walking around Oscar’s space, inspecting pictures and the few trinkets he has around. Suddenly, Oscar’s glad he had the bright idea to tidy up a bit, shoving his clothes into his room and putting away his dirty dishes. 

 

“I try to,” Oscar says. “If not around the city, then on the track. Before everyone does their walks. You should join me, if you want.” 

 

Lando looks at him over his shoulder with a sort of hard, calculating gaze. “Yeah, maybe.” 

 

“Smoothie?” Oscar asks. “Or I can make tea?” 

 

“Tea,” Lando says, perking up a bit at the mention of it. Oscar eyes the way he’s still wrapped up in a jacket. His curls buried under a thick beanie. Oscar’s sweating at the sight of it. More than he already was. “Is this your family?” 

 

Lando points to a framed photo of him and his family from his university graduation ceremony. 

 

“Yeah,” Oscar says as he busies himself finding two clean mugs and bags of tea. “Probably the last time they came to the UK.”

 

Lando hums. “Do you miss them?” 

 

Does he miss them? More than anything. Phone calls are never enough. Not when the time change is too drastic and the free time is too little. 

 

Not as he sees his sisters becoming women in the blink of an eye. His mum getting married to a man he hardly knows. Oscar missing all the milestones of their lives. 

 

“Of course,” he says, filling his kettle. 

 

“You didn't want to go back to Australia? After the crash?”

 

Isn’t that quite the complicated question? 

 

Oscar shrugs.

 

“Sometimes,” he says. “But at the point I was out of racing, it felt like I had been gone too long. And my parents had already paid for me to go to this boarding school, so I figured I might as well finish it out. Met a girl too. Didn’t want to go back home after that.” 

 

“Oh, does she live here too?” Lando asks, eyes searching around for photos of a girl. Or any sign of a girlfriend.

 

“No,” Oscar laughs. “We’re still friends, though. She’s an engineer too, but she ended up going to Cambridge. And I had a Prema engineer who helped me get into Oxford. We sorta just drifted apart.”

 

“That sucks,” Lando says. 

 

“I guess,” Oscar shrugs, watching him wander around. He bites his lip, wondering if he should say what’s on the tip of his tongue for a moment. “Besides, I went to university and realized girls don’t really do it for me.” 

 

Lando pauses before he turns to look at Oscar. He gives him a once-over, his face carefully blank.

 

“Oh,” he says, blandly. “That’s cool.”

 

Oscar lets out a little sigh of relief. He’s heard all the rumors and online talk of Lando being more than interested in men. 

 

It wasn’t something that Oscar believed, hearing more than enough paddock gossip about which new model Lando was hooking up with or inviting to races.  

 

Still, he wasn’t sure if the rumors had made Lando uncomfortable by even associating him with anything less than straight. Usually, Oscar kept his sexuality and his dating life out of his work life. 

 

But something about Lando made Oscar’s mouth just open and start talking. 

 

 Lando makes his way back to where Oscar was. He watches as Oscar fills two mugs with hot water. “So a boyfriend then?” 

 

“No,” Oscar says, laughing. “I was with my ex for most of university, but he couldn't deal with me being gone all the time. And the long-distance thing just didn’t work.”

 

Lando throws his head back and sighs. “Tell me about it, mate. Everyone thinks F1 is so cool, but no one wants to date you when you’re gone half the year.” 

 

Huh. Oscar hadn’t really thought of that. That he and Lando might know the same pain. That they both might have dealt with the same struggles. 

 

“Exactly,” Oscar says. “It’s fine. I like my life. Have a spent a good amount of time being single. I like it. I  feel like I wouldn't want to be with someone unless they truly add to my life at this point. Like they'd have to be really, really special.”

 

Lando peers at him. Those wide, color-shifting eyes searching Oscar’s face. There’s a sort of defeated look on Lando’s face, though Oscar can’t really place why. 

 

“I see,” Lando says, voice a little more distant than it was a moment ago. “So you're not looking then?”

 

“No, just happy with me,” Oscar says. “I guess if it happens, it happens. But otherwise, I’ve created a good life for myself, and I don’t need another person to enjoy it.  What about you? Anyone in your life?” 

 

Lando takes a sip of his tea before he shakes his head. “Nah, just broke it off with my girlfriend.”

 

“Oh, I’m sorry, mate,” Oscar says, and finds he means it based on how downtrodden Lando looks as he says it.

It makes him anxious. Makes him feel out of sorts.

There are a few moments of Oscar figuring out the gears. Of him getting used to the touchiness of the brakes. 

 

But once he does, he looks younger and freer than Lando’s ever seen him. 

 

He’s smiling. 

 

A true, warm smile. Something so unrestrained and light. It makes Lando feel a bit strange. 

 

Strange since Lando can’t help but look at him. Can’t help but take in his excited eyes. The way the setting sun paints him in golden light creates a halo around the swoop of his windswept hair. The way his biceps and veins pop from how he’s holding the steering wheel.

There’s an excited flush that spreads across his cheeks and nose. 

 

He looks over at Lando and smiles at him so brightly. So warm, it makes Lando feel hot all over. 

 

Gorgeous, Lando thinks before he can stop himself. He’s gorgeous when he’s happy.

He made Oscar this happy. This full of glee and excitement.

A deep desire to keep him looking this bright and beautiful. To have him look at Lando like he hung the moon. To have this devastating happiness from Oscar all to himself. 

 

Lando’s still in a bit of a haze as they pull up to their hotel.

 

Oscar leans across the console after he parks and looks at Lando intently. “Thank you, Lando. This was amazing. Truly.” 

 

The words are nothing special. 

 

But Lando does his best to ignore the feeling of pride in himself that they invoke. Confirmation that he did something so good for Oscar. 

 

That he made Oscar feel good.

 

That he wants to do it again and again.

It’s early when Lando wakes up the next morning. So early, the sun is just starting to peek out over the ocean and bring bright stripes of light through his half-open curtains. The air is just starting to warm up with the heat and humidity.

Chapter Summary

Some golf, some dinner, some Montreal

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

It’s just that there aren’t many things Oscar finds himself doing between race weekends. He usually just travels, has a few meals or drinks with his coworkers, and then takes any moment he can to relax. 

 

Sometimes he goes for a run in whatever city they’re in. 

 

Sometimes he lazes around in his bed and catches up on the bad show his sister wants him to watch. 

 

Sometimes he goes sightseeing. 

 

More often than not, he works, slumped over spreadsheets and data from the last race. Trying to learn anything new. Trying to find somewhere for his team to extract any ounce of extra pace. 

 

Very much unlike Lando. Who seemingly slinks off to coastal towns, lounging on yachts and wasting away the day with an endless lineup of friends. Flirting with the entire female population of Portugal. Driving his impressive car collection around Monte Carlo.
